# Test split: the heavy suites by what their tests read

This is part of the target design, not an option. The user ruled that the tests are the main thing the split solves for, so the test modularization lands with the crates. [The target layout](modularization-target-layout.md) gives the crates and command suites, and this document splits the test suites inside it. Both are replayed together on the same 144 PRs below. Like the rest of the design this is design-only: no file under `src/`, `crates/` or `tests/` changes, and no test is removed, sampled or sharded.

## The split

**Which suites.** Every suite holding more than 3% of a full Linux run's estimated test-seconds splits. On the reviewed source those are cmd-start, cross-command, cmd-reply, cmd-results, cmd-status and cmd-adopt. `scripts/modularization/test-split.mjs` derives the split from the target catalog and writes `test-split.json`; its `--check` refuses a file the rule would not produce. Eight e2e modules have no Linux timing in the profile. As in the replay, they carry no weight in the 3% rule, `test-split.json` lists them as `unmeasured`, and their tests are still assigned like any other.

**By what the tests read.** A suite first splits by the source module its tests come from (`cmd-start--lifecycle`). A test reads its own body and its module's helpers, constants and fixtures beside the shared harness, so a module is the unit that historical edits to test code land in. A module part still over 3% (cmd-start's `continuation`, `lifecycle` and `adoption`) is ordered by the set of helpers each test reads and cut at equal estimated seconds (`cmd-start--continuation--1` to `--3`). Tests reading the same helpers stay together. A helper edit still selects every part that reads that helper, and an edit to one test's body selects only its part. Connected components over shared helpers were measured first and do not separate these modules: shared module-local helpers join 41 of continuation's 42 phases, 87 of lifecycle's 95 and all 47 of adoption's into one component each.

**One suite per test.** `checkSplit` assigns every test phase of the target catalog to exactly one suite. It refuses a split that drops a test of a suite it splits, lists a test twice or under a parent it does not belong to, names a test the catalog lacks, or names one part twice. The catalog's own checks already hold every existing e2e test to its phases. Together the 1,922 existing e2e tests each map to the suites their phases move to, and the "Every split suite" table below lists each part's tests.

**What each part depends on.** A command part keeps its command's crates and only the test-fake crates its own tests start or link. A cross-command part links the commands its journeys actually drive through the dispatcher's own routing crates (`cli-schema`, `diagnostics`, `processes`), not through the facade that registers every command. A part keeps the dispatcher and the facade wherever one of its phases calls the public library or carries argv the trace could not resolve. Its inputs are its tests' own code, the fixtures they read, the harness items they call, and the contract sections they parse.

**Harness and test files per item.** A hunk in a test file reaches the parts whose tests or traced helpers contain it, as before. The replay now also attributes these to the item below them: doc comments, `#[test]` and other attributes, and the comments directly above an item. Types and constants a test file declares belong to the owned items whose code names them, to a fixpoint. Whitespace-only lines between items select nothing. Imports, `impl` headers, `Drop` and the `World` struct remain whole-file inputs, because every user of the file reads them. The table of broad PRs below names where that still forces a run, and the design change that would remove it.

**Contract sections.** A test reading `docs/contract.md`, `docs/contract-divergences.md` or `docs/stop-guard.md` locates its passage by literal needles: a divergence entry by its numbered heading, or a block by a key it contains. `contract-sections.mjs` resolves those needles at the reviewed commit to a heading-bounded section. A needle that names no single section must fall inside the section the others name, or the read keeps the whole document. Such a read is selected only when its section's text changes, or when its needles stop naming exactly one section, in the PR's before-and-after documents. Reads with no resolving needle keep the whole file, and the table below lists them.

**How it runs.** Each part is a nextest filterset over its parent's runner binary (`-E 'test(/^lifecycle::/)'` for a module part, the listed test names for a cut part). The runner count, and so the target layout's compile estimate, is unchanged, and no test's code changes to fit a part. Per-item and per-section inputs become Nx inputs the way the target layout's configuration fingerprints do: a materializer writes one fingerprint per harness item, test-file item and scoped section, and each part's test target takes the fingerprints of what it reads. Where attribution is uncertain the input stays the whole file.

## What it measures, candidly

The split moves estimated Linux seconds skipped from the crate-only 6.39% to the figure in the first generated table. Test code is no longer the leading cause. Most PRs, though, still select nearly every second, through inputs no test split can narrow. The largest is a changed lockfile or manifest resolution for crates every heavy part links. Shared engine source and harness items outside one helper follow. The "What still selects the seconds" table ranks them.

The decision summary below counts the PRs still selecting at least 90% of the seconds by the cause that forces them, says which causes a further split could free, and gives the upper bound each would reach. Even all three such splits together, harness.rs into per-concern files, each part's tests into their own file and the engine files physically divided, leave most of those PRs selecting at least 90%. What forces them is genuine: shared engine code, a crate's resolved dependencies (mostly sibling-engine bumps) and its manifest requirements.

**Why sibling-only lockfile bumps select nearly everything.** The user asked why a lockfile change moving only sibling-engine packages selects at least 90% of the seconds, when only some crates use those engines. The decision summary answers from the replay, path by path. One path was a model artifact. Production crates' external roots counted the imports of their own `#[cfg(test)]` unit-test modules, so `plan-validation` and `plan-edits` appeared to link the test-fake package and, through it, every engine its fakes use. Those imports are dev-dependencies, which reach no dependent and no e2e suite, so the target model now excludes them. That moved the PRs this cause forces from 41 to 40 and left the published shares unchanged at two decimals. The rest is real. The foundational engine crates (`plan-model`, `plan-edits`, `publication`, `event-payloads`, `publication-boundary`) name sibling types in their production code, and nearly every command reaches them. Separately, the suites' own test code calls the engines in-process: the harness registers every checkout through `onevcs`, reads a schema constant from `oneagentgraph`, and drives task stores through `onetaskgraph-core`. The agent and host fakes link the engines they act out. Confining the engines behind adapter crates, with the shared crates holding their own value types, frees only part of this, as the summary's projection shows, because the test code's own dependencies stay.

The decision summary's "Why a typical PR still selects nearly every second" subsection names what the typical PR changed and the crates that suites holding at least 90% of the seconds all reach. No split of the suites removes those crates. The 30-minute line is not reached by selection under this design. The wall-clock table holds each measured job's non-e2e residual fixed and scales its e2e tier by the PR's selected share. Typical PRs project to today's full sweep on every platform, because the median PR still selects nearly all the seconds, and the Linux job's residual alone is over 30 minutes. Reaching the line needs per-test and per-job cost reduction, which the parallel `onepipeline-test-cost` work owns, or a coverage trade-off that only the user can make. These measurements were sent to the manager. The estimates divide each module's measured seconds evenly among its tests, and the job measurements are full sweeps of today's tree on run 40947970908. Neither is a benchmark of the future layout.

## Migration

The test-split steps follow the crate steps of the target layout and land one at a time; the generated table gives the skip share after each.

1. **Project test inputs per item and section.** Add the fingerprint materializer for harness items, test-file items and scoped contract sections, and give each existing suite the fingerprints its tests read in place of the whole files. No test moves.
2. **Split each heavy suite, heaviest first:** cmd-start, cross-command, cmd-reply, cmd-results, cmd-status, then cmd-adopt. Each step adds one Nx test target per part, running that part's filterset over the unchanged runner binary, and removes the parent's single target in the same change.
3. **Re-home start's, reply's and adopt's engine assertions** (steps 8 to 10), as [the engine re-homing](modularization-engine-tests.md) designs and replays them.

For every step, the binary is untouched, so its behaviour and the published crate's public API are what the crate steps left them. Every test keeps its assertions and runs in exactly one part, because `test-split.mjs --check` runs over the new targets' filters before the parent target is removed, and nextest's own listing of each part, unioned, must equal the parent's. No test is removed, sampled or sharded across jobs: a part runs in the same job its parent did.

## Reproduce

```sh
node scripts/modularization/pinned.mjs --write-back -- node scripts/modularization/test-split.mjs --write
node scripts/modularization/pinned.mjs -- node scripts/modularization/split-replay.mjs "$ONEPIPELINE_NODE_SCRATCH_DIR/split-replay.json"
node scripts/modularization/pinned.mjs --write-back -- node scripts/modularization/split-report.mjs "$ONEPIPELINE_NODE_SCRATCH_DIR/split-replay.json"
just nx run onepipeline-modularization:check
```

`ci-wall.json` records the measured jobs: their run, job ids, job seconds and e2e tier seconds, read from the jobs' timestamps and the e2e tier's nextest summary line.

## Projected job wall clock against the 30-minute line

The first three columns are **measured** on run 40947970908; the rest are **projected**. Typical is the median PR, worst is the PR selecting the most estimated seconds. A projection holds the measured job less its e2e tier fixed and scales the e2e tier by the PR's selected share; the stress columns add the target layout's all-runner compile stress. Windows is one job whose e2e tier is both of today's legs added: a second leg would be sharding, which does not count toward the line.

Local gate test phase: 10294 s (171.6 min) **measured** (the profile's run F); **projected** typical 10285 s (171.4 min), worst 10294 s (171.6 min). This scales only the test phase; the gate's compilation and other tiers are not modelled.

## Decision summary

**Projected** (replay model): 122 of 144 PRs still select at least 90% of a full run's estimated Linux seconds. A PR counts under every input kind whose own inputs alone select at least 90%; one with no such kind counts under "several inputs together". The skip shares treat that kind as selecting nothing: an upper bound where a further split could free it, and only a measure of its weight where no design change removes it.

| Forcing input kind | PRs it forces (of the broad PRs) | PRs it alone forces | Further design change that would free it | Suite runs skipped if freed | Linux seconds skipped if freed | PRs still at 90% or more |
| --- | ---: | ---: | --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| engine source | 74 | 7 | no: shared engine code changed | 14.29% | 12.79% | 115 |
| engine source outside any attributed item | 66 | 5 | by a further split: the file physically divided along the subsystem boundaries, so a hunk outside every item lands in one crate's file | 17.74% | 17.12% | 117 |
| lockfile resolution | 56 | 2 | no: a crate's resolved dependencies changed | 13.56% | 12.75% | 120 |
| manifest requirement | 53 | 1 | no: a crate's dependency requirement changed | 12.16% | 11.33% | 122 |
| test configuration section | 16 | 4 | no: the section every runner reads changed | 14.95% | 14.13% | 118 |
| harness item outside one helper | 14 | 0 | by a further split: harness.rs into per-concern files, so World's core, its Drop and its imports stop sharing a file with the store and hook helpers (helpers move; no assertion changes) | 12.18% | 11.35% | 122 |
| workspace tooling | 12 | 3 | no: Nx and its lockfile are inputs of every target | 14.25% | 13.40% | 119 |
| harness helper | 11 | 0 | no: a helper its users call changed | 12.17% | 11.34% | 122 |
| test fake | 11 | 1 | no: a fake every reaching suite starts | 12.43% | 11.57% | 121 |
| test file outside one test | 2 | 1 | by a further split: each part's tests in their own file, so a module's imports and added tests reach only that part (tests move; no assertion changes) | 12.54% | 11.80% | 121 |
| every kind a further split could free (harness item outside one helper; test file outside one test; engine source outside any attributed item) | | | all three splits together | 19.35% | 18.83% | 115 |

**Measured** (each PR's own parent and merge lockfiles) for the 56 broad PRs a lockfile resolution forces; sibling engines are the `one*` packages this repository links:

| Lockfile change | PRs |
| --- | ---: |
| sibling-engine packages only | 40 |
| third-party packages only | 2 |
| both | 14 |

### Why a sibling-only lockfile change selects nearly everything

**Projected** (replay model) for the 40 broad PRs whose lockfile change moved only sibling-engine packages and whose lockfile resolution alone selects at least 90% of Linux seconds. This workspace's own `onepipeline*` packages are not counted as a moved dependency. A crate's resolved set changes when a package reachable from its own external roots moved; a suite is selected when any crate in its runner's closure changed. The seconds columns are each path's Linux suite-seconds, summed over the 40 PRs as a share of their full runs: "alone" is what that path's crates select by themselves, "only through this path" is what no other path also selects.

| Path to the moved engine | PRs | Linux seconds it selects alone | Linux seconds only through this path |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| a shared engine crate the suite reaches transitively, through its command's dependencies | 40 | 98.47% | 13.23% |
| the test-fake crates the suite starts or links | 40 | 75.84% | 0.00% |
| the suite's own test code (its runner's imports) | 40 | 58.52% | 0.00% |
| the suite's own command crate | 29 | 17.67% | 0.00% |
| the dispatcher or facade | 1 | 0.04% | 0.00% |

Of the 278 crates whose resolved set changed in any of these PRs, 278 name a moved engine in their own production code (or, for a runner, in the test code its phases reach), 74 also reach it through another sibling that depends on it (a crate may do both), and 0 reach it only through a third-party package. No path in these PRs runs through a third-party package the bump also moved: by selection, these lockfile changes moved sibling packages only.

| #848 / 70bb2260 | onetaskgraph-core, onetaskgraph-github-projects, onetaskgraph-in-memory, onetaskgraph-linear, onetaskgraph-local-md, onetaskgraph-plugin-api | 9 | 0 | 84 | 2 | 0 | 98.47% |
| #852 / a43e3e49 | onevcs, onevcs-testing | 30 | 7 | 158 | 4 | 0 | 99.97% |
| #866 / cce8e7f8 | onevcs, onevcs-testing | 30 | 7 | 158 | 4 | 0 | 99.97% |
| #828 / f98a6316 | onevcs, onevcs-testing | 30 | 7 | 158 | 4 | 0 | 99.97% |
| #884 / aec76676 | onetaskgraph-core, onetaskgraph-github-projects, onetaskgraph-in-memory, onetaskgraph-linear, onetaskgraph-local-md, onetaskgraph-plugin-api | 9 | 0 | 84 | 2 | 0 | 98.47% |
| #886 / c1f8e4cf | onevcs, onevcs-testing | 30 | 7 | 158 | 4 | 0 | 99.97% |
| #909 / ce8595d6 | onevcs, onevcs-testing | 30 | 7 | 158 | 4 | 0 | 99.97% |

**The design change, projected as an upper bound:** shared engine crates hold their own value types where today they embed a sibling's, and reach the engines only behind adapter crates the command crates wire, so a sibling bump changes only the crates that call that engine. Keeping sibling resolution only in command crates, runners, test fakes and the dispatcher, the replay reaches 13.15% of suite runs and 11.93% of Linux seconds skipped (from 12.15% and 11.32%). 37 of these 40 PRs and 119 of all 144 would still select at least 90% of the seconds, because the suites' own test code and their fakes call the engines themselves: the harness registers every checkout through `onevcs` in-process, reads `oneagentgraph`'s task-token schema version and drives task stores through `onetaskgraph-core`, and the agent, host and harness fakes link the engines they act out. Those are real dependencies of realistic tests, which this design does not replace.

## Where each job's residual goes

**Measured** on run 40947970908. Segments add up to the job; Linux's quality-gate tasks run concurrently, so its task durations overlap and are listed apart.

**linux** (step seconds from the job's steps; inside the quality-gate step Nx flushes every task's output at the end, so task spans come from the per-test start stamps in the job's test-profile-gate artifact and task durations from each tool's own summary line):

| Segment | Seconds |
| --- | ---: |
| runner setup steps (checkout, caches, toolchains) | 27.0 |
| bootstrap | 302.0 |
| quality gate before the e2e tier starts: the instrumented test-archive build and the tasks Nx runs beside it | 1709.0 |
| e2e tier | 3502.8 |
| quality gate after the e2e tier | 926.2 |
| steps after the quality gate (profile records, failed-test re-run, cleanup) | 7.0 |

Task durations the Linux job log reports, overlapping the segments above: onepipeline-npm:test 1467 s, onepipeline:test-rest 404 s, onepipeline-note-journeys:test 296 s, onepipeline-stop-verdict:test 83 s, onepipeline-codex-server:test 66 s, onepipeline:budgets 66 s. Not reported in the job log: the instrumented test-archive build, format-check, lint, and the contract and modularization tests, whose durations the job log does not carry.

## What still selects the seconds

**Projected** (replay model): each selected suite-second is charged evenly to the kinds of input that selected that suite in that PR.

| Input kind | Share of all Linux suite-seconds the 144 PRs would run | PRs | Removed by a design change |
| --- | ---: | ---: | --- |
| engine source outside any attributed item | 25.19% | 105 | by a further split: the file physically divided along the subsystem boundaries, so a hunk outside every item lands in one crate's file |
| engine source | 18.45% | 85 | no: shared engine code changed |
| lockfile resolution | 11.94% | 56 | no: a crate's resolved dependencies changed |
| manifest requirement | 10.22% | 53 | no: a crate's dependency requirement changed |
| test configuration section | 5.27% | 16 | no: the section every runner reads changed |
| test fake | 4.30% | 41 | no: a fake every reaching suite starts |
| workspace tooling | 3.86% | 12 | no: Nx and its lockfile are inputs of every target |
| harness helper | 2.56% | 32 | no: a helper its users call changed |
| harness item outside one helper | 2.50% | 14 | by a further split: harness.rs into per-concern files, so World's core, its Drop and its imports stop sharing a file with the store and hook helpers (helpers move; no assertion changes) |
| test file outside one test | 2.16% | 83 | by a further split: each part's tests in their own file, so a module's imports and added tests reach only that part (tests move; no assertion changes) |
| document read whole | 1.36% | 74 | only by a test edit giving the read a needle that names its section |
| test code | 0.64% | 84 | no: the part's own tests changed |
| other | 0.24% | 37 | no |

## Every split suite and the tests it runs

Linux seconds per run are **projected** (the profile's module seconds divided evenly). Each part runs as a nextest filter over its parent's runner binary, so no runner is added. Runner crates are the crates the part's tests reach; a cross-command part links only the commands its journeys drive, and keeps the dispatcher and facade where a phase uses the public API or has unresolved argv.

| Suite | Parent | Tests | Linux s per run | Runner crates | Tests it runs |
